                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |

**Aristotle**[\[A\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-2) ([Attic Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attic_Greek_language "Attic Greek language"): Ἀριστοτέλης, romanized: *Aristotélēs*;[\[B\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-3) 384–322 BC) was an [Ancient Greek philosopher](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_philosophy "Ancient Greek philosophy") and [polymath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polymath "Polymath"). His writings cover a broad range of subjects spanning the [natural sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_science "Natural science"), [philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philosophy "Philosophy"), [linguistics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Linguistics "Linguistics"), [economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics "Economics"), [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics "Politics"), [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology "Psychology"), and [the arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_arts "The arts"). As the founder of the [Peripatetic school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripatetic_school "Peripatetic school") of philosophy in the [Lyceum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_\(classical\) "Lyceum (classical)") in [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Athens "Athens"), he began the wider [Aristotelian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelianism "Aristotelianism") tradition that followed, which set the groundwork for the development of modern [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science "Science").

Little is known about Aristotle's life. He was born in the city of [Stagira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagira_\(ancient_city\) "Stagira (ancient city)") in [northern Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Greece "Northern Greece") during the [Classical period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Greece "Classical Greece"). His father, [Nicomachus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachus_\(father_of_Aristotle\) "Nicomachus (father of Aristotle)"), died when Aristotle was a child, and he was brought up by a guardian. At around eighteen years old, he joined [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato")'s [Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platonic_Academy "Platonic Academy") in Athens and remained there until the age of thirty seven (c. 347 BC). Shortly after Plato died, Aristotle left Athens and, at the request of [Philip II of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon "Philip II of Macedon"), tutored his son [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great") beginning in 343 BC. He established a library in the Lyceum, which helped him to produce many of his hundreds of books on [papyrus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papyrus "Papyrus") [scrolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scrolls "Scrolls").

Though Aristotle wrote many treatises and dialogues for publication, only around [a third of his original output has survived](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Works_of_Aristotle "Works of Aristotle"), none of it intended for publication. Aristotle provided a complex synthesis of the various philosophies existing prior to him. His teachings and methods of inquiry have had a significant impact across the world, and remain a subject of contemporary philosophical discussion.

Aristotle's views profoundly shaped [medieval scholarship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_science#Middle_Ages "History of science"). The influence of [his physical science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_physics "Aristotelian physics") extended from [late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_antiquity "Late antiquity") and the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages "Early Middle Ages") into the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance "Renaissance"), and was not replaced systematically until [the Enlightenment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment "Age of Enlightenment") and theories such as [classical mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_mechanics "Classical mechanics") were developed. He influenced [Judeo-Islamic philosophies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judeo-Islamic_philosophies_\(800%E2%80%931400\) "Judeo-Islamic philosophies (800–1400)") during the Middle Ages, as well as [Christian theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_theology "Christian theology"), especially the [Neoplatonism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoplatonism "Neoplatonism") of the [Early Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Church "Early Church") and the [scholastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism "Scholasticism") tradition of the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church").

Aristotle was revered among medieval Muslim scholars as "The First Teacher", and among medieval Christians like [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas "Thomas Aquinas") as simply "The Philosopher", while the poet [Dante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Alighieri "Dante Alighieri") called him "the master of those who know". He has been referred to as the first [scientist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientist "Scientist"). His works contain the earliest known formal study of logic, and were studied by medieval scholars such as [Peter Abelard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Abelard "Peter Abelard") and [Jean Buridan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Buridan "Jean Buridan"). His influence on logic continued well into the 19th century. In addition, [his ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_ethics "Aristotelian ethics"), although always influential, has gained renewed interest with the modern advent of [virtue ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virtue_ethics "Virtue ethics").

## Life

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Life")]

In general, the details of Aristotle's life are not well-established. The biographies written in ancient times are often speculative and historians only agree on a few salient points.[\[C\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-4) Aristotle was born in 384 BC[\[D\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-5) in [Stagira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stagira_\(ancient_city\) "Stagira (ancient city)"), [Chalcidice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcidice "Chalcidice"),[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-6) about 55 km (34 miles) east of modern-day [Thessaloniki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thessaloniki "Thessaloniki").[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-7)[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristoteles-Park_in_Stagira-8) He was the son of [Nicomachus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachus_\(father_of_Aristotle\) "Nicomachus (father of Aristotle)"), the personal physician of [King Amyntas of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amyntas_III_of_Macedon "Amyntas III of Macedon"),[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-9) and Phaestis, a woman with origins from [Chalcis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcis "Chalcis"), [Euboea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euboea "Euboea").[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10) Nicomachus was said to have belonged to the medical guild of [Asclepiadae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asclepiad_\(title\) "Asclepiad (title)") and was likely responsible for Aristotle's early interest in biology and medicine.[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-11) Ancient tradition held that Aristotle's family descended from the legendary physician [Asclepius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asclepius "Asclepius") and his son [Machaon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machaon_\(mythology\) "Machaon (mythology)").[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-12) Both of Aristotle's parents died when he was still at a young age and [Proxenus of Atarneus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proxenus_of_Atarneus "Proxenus of Atarneus") became his guardian.[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-13) Although little information about Aristotle's childhood has survived, he probably spent some time in the [Macedonian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(ancient_kingdom\) "Macedonia (ancient kingdom)") capital, making his first connections with the [Macedonian monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Argead_dynasty "Argead dynasty").[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-14)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e5/20160518_092_mieza_nympheum.jpg/230px-20160518_092_mieza_nympheum.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:20160518_092_mieza_nympheum.jpg)

School of Aristotle in [Mieza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieza,_Macedonia "Mieza, Macedonia"), [Macedonia, Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macedonia_\(Greece\) "Macedonia (Greece)").

At the age of seventeen or eighteen, Aristotle moved to [Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_Athens "Classical Athens") to continue his education at [Plato's Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Platonic_Academy "Platonic Academy").[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-15) He became distinguished as a researcher and lecturer, earning for himself the nickname "mind of the school" by his tutor [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato").[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHazel201337-16) In Athens, he probably experienced the [Eleusinian Mysteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eleusinian_Mysteries "Eleusinian Mysteries") as he wrote when describing the sights one viewed at the Mysteries, "to experience is to learn" (παθεĩν μαθεĩν).[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEvans200618-17) Aristotle remained in Athens for nearly twenty years before leaving in 348/47 BC after Plato's death.[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) The traditional story about his departure records that he was disappointed with the academy's direction after control passed to Plato's nephew [Speusippus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speusippus "Speusippus"), although it is possible that the anti-Macedonian sentiments in Athens could have also influenced his decision.[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENussbaumOsborne201473-19)[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle1984Introduction-20) Aristotle left with [Xenocrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenocrates "Xenocrates") to [Assos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assos "Assos") in [Asia Minor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asia_Minor "Asia Minor"), where he was invited by his former fellow student [Hermias of Atarneus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hermias_of_Atarneus "Hermias of Atarneus"); he stayed there for a few years and left around the time of Hermias' death.[\[E\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-21) While at Assos, Aristotle and his colleague [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus "Theophrastus") did extensive research in [botany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botany "Botany") and [marine biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_biology "Marine biology"), which they later continued at the near-by island of [Lesbos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesbos "Lesbos").[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22) During this time, Aristotle married [Pythias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythias "Pythias"), Hermias's adoptive daughter and niece, and had a daughter whom they also named Pythias.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields20126-23)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f2/Aristotle_tutoring_Alexander.jpg/174px-Aristotle_tutoring_Alexander.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotle_tutoring_Alexander.jpg)

"Aristotle tutoring Alexander" (1895) by [Jean Leon Gerome Ferris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean_Leon_Gerome_Ferris "Jean Leon Gerome Ferris").

In 343/42 BC, Aristotle was invited to [Pella](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pella "Pella") by [Philip II of Macedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Macedon "Philip II of Macedon") in order to become the tutor to his thirteen-year-old son [Alexander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great");[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24) a choice perhaps influenced by the relationship of Aristotle's family with the Macedonian dynasty.[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25) Aristotle taught Alexander at the private school of [Mieza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mieza_\(Macedonia\) "Mieza (Macedonia)"), in the gardens of the [Nymphs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nymph "Nymph"), the royal estate near Pella.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-26) Alexander's education probably included a number of subjects, such as [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics "Ethics") and [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics "Politics"),[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-27) as well as standard literary texts, like [Euripides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euripides "Euripides") and [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer "Homer").[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-28) It is likely that during Aristotle's time in the Macedonian court, other prominent nobles, like [Ptolemy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemy_I_Soter "Ptolemy I Soter") and [Cassander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cassander "Cassander"), would have occasionally attended his lectures.[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29) Aristotle encouraged Alexander toward eastern conquest, and his own attitude towards [Persia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Achaemenid_Empire "Achaemenid Empire") was strongly [ethnocentric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnocentricism "Ethnocentricism"). In one famous example, he counsels Alexander to be "a leader to the Greeks and a despot to the barbarians".[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30) Alexander's education under the guardianship of Aristotle likely lasted for only a few years, as at around the age of sixteen he returned to Pella and was appointed regent of Macedon by his father Philip.[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31) During this time, Aristotle is said to have gifted Alexander an annotated copy of the *[Iliad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iliad "Iliad")*, which reportedly became one of Alexander's most prized possessions.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32) Scholars speculate that two of Aristotle's now lost works, *On kingship* and *On behalf of the Colonies*, were composed by the philosopher for the young prince.[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-33) After Philip II's assassination in 336 BC, Aristotle returned to Athens for the second and final time a year later.[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields20127-34)

As a [metic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metic "Metic"), Aristotle could not own property in Athens and thus rented a building known as the [Lyceum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_\(classical\) "Lyceum (classical)") (named after the sacred [grove](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grove_\(nature\) "Grove (nature)") of [Apollo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apollo "Apollo") *Lykeios*), in which he established his own school.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-35) The building included a [gymnasium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gymnasium_\(ancient_Greece\) "Gymnasium (ancient Greece)") and a [colonnade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonnade "Colonnade") (*peripatos*), from which the school acquired the name *[Peripatetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripatetic_school "Peripatetic school")*.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-36) Aristotle conducted courses and research at the school for the next twelve years. He often lectured small groups of distinguished students and, along with some of them, such as [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus "Theophrastus"), [Eudemus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eudemus_of_Rhodes "Eudemus of Rhodes"), and [Aristoxenus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristoxenus "Aristoxenus"), Aristotle built a large library which included manuscripts, maps, and museum objects.[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-37) While in Athens, his wife Pythias died and Aristotle became involved with [Herpyllis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herpyllis "Herpyllis") of Stagira. They had a son whom Aristotle named after his father, [Nicomachus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachus_\(son_of_Aristotle\) "Nicomachus (son of Aristotle)").[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) This period in Athens, between 335 and 323 BC, is when Aristotle is believed to have composed many of his philosophical works.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-39) He wrote many dialogues, of which only fragments have survived. Those works that have survived are in [treatise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treatise "Treatise") form and were not, for the most part, intended for widespread publication; they are generally thought to be lecture aids for his students. His most important treatises include *[Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_\(Aristotle\) "Physics (Aristotle)")*, *[Metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics_\(Aristotle\) "Metaphysics (Aristotle)")*, *[Nicomachean Ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachean_Ethics "Nicomachean Ethics")*, *[Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_\(Aristotle\) "Politics (Aristotle)")*, *[On the Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Soul "On the Soul")* and *[Poetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetics_\(Aristotle\) "Poetics (Aristotle)")*. Aristotle studied and made significant contributions to "logic, metaphysics, mathematics, physics, biology, botany, ethics, politics, agriculture, medicine, dance, and theatre."[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHumphreys2009-40)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a4/Aristoteles_Louvre.jpg/170px-Aristoteles_Louvre.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristoteles_Louvre.jpg)

[Portrait bust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bust_\(sculpture\) "Bust (sculpture)") of Aristotle; an [Imperial Roman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire "Roman Empire") (1st or 2nd century AD) copy of a lost [bronze sculpture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_sculpture "Bronze sculpture") made by [Lysippos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysippos "Lysippos").

While Alexander deeply admired Aristotle, near the end of his life, the two men became estranged having diverging opinions over issues, like the optimal administration of city-states, the treatment of conquered populations, such as the Persians, and philosophical questions, like the definition of braveness.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWu202272%E2%80%9374-41) A widespread speculation in antiquity suggested that Aristotle played a role in Alexander's death, but the only evidence of this is an [unlikely claim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hagnothemis "Hagnothemis") made some six years after the death.[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGreen1991460-42) Following Alexander's death, anti-Macedonian sentiment in Athens was rekindled. In 322 BC, Demophilus and [Eurymedon the Hierophant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurymedon_the_Hierophant "Eurymedon the Hierophant") reportedly denounced Aristotle for impiety,[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFilonik201372%E2%80%9373-43) prompting him to flee to his mother's family estate in [Chalcis, Euboea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chalcis "Chalcis"), at which occasion he was said to have stated "I will not allow the Athenians to sin twice against philosophy"[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-44) – a reference to Athens's [trial and execution of Socrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trial_of_Socrates "Trial of Socrates").[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENussbaumOsborne201473-19) He died in Chalcis, Euboea[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle_\(Greek_philosopher\)-45)[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46) of natural causes later that same year, having named his student [Antipater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antipater "Antipater") as his chief [executor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executor "Executor") and leaving a [will](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Will_\(law\) "Will (law)") in which he asked to be buried next to his wife.[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHaase19923862-47) Aristotle left his works to Theophrastus, his successor as the head of the Lyceum, who in turn passed them down to [Neleus of Scepsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neleus_of_Scepsis "Neleus of Scepsis") in Asia Minor. There, the papers remained hidden for protection until they were purchased by the collector [Apellicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apellicon "Apellicon"). In the meantime, many copies of Aristotle's major works had already begun to circulate and be used in the Lyceum of Athens, [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria "Alexandria"), and later in [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rome "Rome").[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-48)

## Theoretical philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: Theoretical philosophy")]

### Logic

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Logic")]

Main article: [Term logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Term_logic "Term logic")

Further information: [Non-Aristotelian logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aristotelian_logic "Non-Aristotelian logic")

With the *[Prior Analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prior_Analytics "Prior Analytics")*, Aristotle is credited with the earliest study of formal logic,[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDegnan199481%E2%80%9389-49) and his conception of it was the dominant form of Western logic until 19th-century advances in [mathematical logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematical_logic "Mathematical logic").[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECorcoran20091%E2%80%9320-50) [Kant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Kant "Immanuel Kant") stated in the *[Critique of Pure Reason](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critique_of_Pure_Reason "Critique of Pure Reason")* that with Aristotle, logic reached its completion.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKant1787Preface-51)

#### *Organon*

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Organon")]

Main article: [Organon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organon "Organon")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/98/Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg/170px-Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sanzio_01_Plato_Aristotle.jpg)

[Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato") (left) and Aristotle in [Raphael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael "Raphael")'s 1509 fresco, *[The School of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_School_of_Athens "The School of Athens")*. Aristotle holds his *[Nicomachean Ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachean_Ethics "Nicomachean Ethics")* and gestures to the earth, representing his view in immanent realism, whilst Plato gestures to the heavens, indicating his Theory of Forms, and holds his *[Timaeus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timaeus_\(dialogue\) "Timaeus (dialogue)")*.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESchool_of_Athens-52)[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStewart2019-53)

Most of Aristotle's work is probably not in its original form, because it was most likely edited by students and later lecturers. The logical works of Aristotle were compiled into a set of six books called the *[Organon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organon "Organon")* around 40 BC by [Andronicus of Rhodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronicus_of_Rhodes "Andronicus of Rhodes") or others among his followers.[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPickover200952-54) The books are:

1. *[Categories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Categories_\(Aristotle\) "Categories (Aristotle)")*
2. *[On Interpretation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Interpretation "On Interpretation")*
3. *[Prior Analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prior_Analytics "Prior Analytics")*
4. *[Posterior Analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Posterior_Analytics "Posterior Analytics")*
5. *[Topics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Topics_\(Aristotle\) "Topics (Aristotle)")*
6. *[On Sophistical Refutations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Sophistical_Refutations "On Sophistical Refutations")*

The order of the books (or the teachings from which they are composed) is not certain, but this list was derived from analysis of Aristotle's writings. It goes from the basics, the analysis of simple terms in the *Categories,* the analysis of propositions and their elementary relations in *On Interpretation*, to the study of more complex forms, namely, syllogisms (in the *Analytics*)[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrior_Analytics24b18%E2%80%9320-55)[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBobzien2015-56) and dialectics (in the *Topics* and *Sophistical Refutations*). The first three treatises form the core of the logical theory *stricto sensu*: the grammar of the language of logic and the correct rules of reasoning. The *Rhetoric* is not conventionally included, but it states that it relies on the *Topics*.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2017-57)

| In words                                                                                                                                  | In terms[\[G\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-60) | In equations[\[H\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-62)                                                                                                                                   |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
|     All men are mortal.     All Greeks are men. [∴](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Therefore_sign "Therefore sign") All Greeks are mortal. | M a P S a M S a P                                            | [![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/23/Modus_Barbara_Equations.svg/150px-Modus_Barbara_Equations.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Modus_Barbara_Equations.svg) |

What is today called *Aristotelian logic* with its [types of syllogism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syllogism#Types "Syllogism") (methods of logical argument),[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELagerlund2016-58) Aristotle himself would have labelled "analytics". The term "logic" he reserved to mean *dialectics*.

### Metaphysics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Metaphysics")]

Main article: [Metaphysics (Aristotle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Metaphysics_\(Aristotle\) "Metaphysics (Aristotle)")

The word "metaphysics" appears to have been coined by the first century AD editor who assembled various small selections of Aristotle's works to create the treatise we know by the name *Metaphysics*.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000-63) Aristotle called it "first philosophy", and distinguished it from mathematics and natural science (physics) as the contemplative (*theoretikē*) philosophy which is "theological" and studies the divine. He wrote in his *Metaphysics* (1026a16):

> If there were no other independent things besides the composite natural ones, the study of nature would be the primary kind of knowledge; but if there is some motionless independent thing, the knowledge of this precedes it and is first philosophy, and it is universal *in just this way*, because it is first. And it belongs to this sort of philosophy to study being as being, both what it is and what belongs to it just by virtue of being.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle1999111-64)

#### Substance

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Substance")]

Further information: [Hylomorphism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hylomorphism "Hylomorphism")

Aristotle examines the concepts of [substance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Substance_theory "Substance theory") (*ousia*) and [essence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essence "Essence") (*to ti ên einai*, "the what it was to be") in his *Metaphysics* (Book VII), and he concludes that a particular substance is a combination of both matter and form, a philosophical theory called [hylomorphism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hylomorphism "Hylomorphism"). In Book VIII, he distinguishes the matter of the substance as the [substratum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Material_substratum "Material substratum"), or the stuff of which it is composed. For example, the matter of a house is the bricks, stones, timbers, etc., or whatever constitutes the *potential* house, while the form of the substance is the *actual* house, namely 'covering for bodies and chattels' or any other [differentia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genus-differentia_definition "Genus-differentia definition") that let us define something as a house. The formula that gives the components is the account of the matter, and the formula that gives the differentia is the account of the form.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetaphysicsVIII_1043a_10%E2%80%9330-65)[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000-63)

##### Immanent realism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Immanent realism")]

Main article: [Aristotle's theory of universals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle%27s_theory_of_universals "Aristotle's theory of universals")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Platonic_and_Aristotelian_Forms.svg/330px-Platonic_and_Aristotelian_Forms.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Platonic_and_Aristotelian_Forms.svg)

[Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato")'s [forms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_forms "Theory of forms") exist as [universals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universals "Universals"), like the ideal form of an apple. For Aristotle, both matter and form belong to the individual thing ([hylomorphism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hylomorphism "Hylomorphism")).

Like his teacher Plato, Aristotle's philosophy aims at the [universal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Problem_of_universals "Problem of universals"). Aristotle's [ontology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontology "Ontology") places the universal (*katholou*) in [particulars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particular "Particular") (*kath' hekaston*), things in the world, whereas for Plato the universal is a separately existing form which actual things imitate. For Aristotle, "form" is still what [phenomena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phenomena "Phenomena") are based on, but is "instantiated" in a particular substance.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000-63)

Plato argued that all things have a [universal form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_forms "Theory of forms"), which could be either a property or a relation to other things. When one looks at an apple, for example, one sees an apple, and one can also analyse a form of an apple. In this distinction, there is a particular apple and a universal form of an apple. Moreover, one can place an apple next to a book, so that one can speak of both the book and apple as being next to each other. Plato argued that there are some universal forms that are not a part of particular things. For example, it is possible that there is no particular good in existence, but "good" is still a proper universal form. Aristotle disagreed with Plato on this point, arguing that all universals are instantiated at some period of time, and that there are no universals that are unattached to existing things. In addition, Aristotle disagreed with Plato about the location of universals. Where Plato spoke of the forms as existing separately from the things that participate in them, Aristotle maintained that universals exist within each thing on which each universal is predicated. So, according to Aristotle, the form of apple exists within each apple, rather than in the world of the forms.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000-63)[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd196843%E2%80%9347-66)

##### Potentiality and actuality

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: Potentiality and actuality")]

Concerning the nature of change (*[kinesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potentiality_and_actuality "Potentiality and actuality")*) and its causes, as he outlines in his *[Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_\(Aristotle\) "Physics (Aristotle)")* and *[On Generation and Corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Generation_and_Corruption "On Generation and Corruption") (*&#x33;19b–320a), he distinguishes coming-to-be (*genesis*, also translated as 'generation') from:

1. growth and diminution, which is change in quantity;
2. locomotion, which is change in space; and
3. alteration, which is change in quality.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4a/Flute-player_dolphin_Alcesti_Group_MAN.jpg/220px-Flute-player_dolphin_Alcesti_Group_MAN.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Flute-player_dolphin_Alcesti_Group_MAN.jpg)

Aristotle argued that a capability like playing the flute could be acquired – [the potential made actual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potentiality_and_actuality_\(Aristotle\) "Potentiality and actuality (Aristotle)") – by learning.

Coming-to-be is a change where the substrate of the thing that has undergone the change has itself changed. In that particular change he introduces the concept of potentiality (*[dynamis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dunamis "Dunamis")*) and actuality (*[entelecheia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entelecheia "Entelecheia")*) in association with the matter and the form. Referring to potentiality, this is what a thing is capable of doing or being acted upon if the conditions are right and it is not prevented by something else. For example, the seed of a plant in the soil is potentially (*dynamei*) a plant, and if it is not prevented by something, it will become a plant. Potentially, beings can either 'act' (*poiein*) or 'be acted upon' (*paschein*), which can be either innate or learned. For example, the eyes possess the potentiality of sight (innate – being acted upon), while the capability of playing the flute can be possessed by learning (exercise – acting). Actuality is the fulfilment of the end of the potentiality. Because the end (*telos*) is the principle of every change, and potentiality exists for the sake of the end, actuality, accordingly, is the end. Referring then to the previous example, it can be said that an actuality is when a plant does one of the activities that plants do.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000-63)

> For that for the sake of which (*to hou heneka*) a thing is, is its principle, and the becoming is for the sake of the end; and the actuality is the end, and it is for the sake of this that the potentiality is acquired. For animals do not see in order that they may have sight, but they have sight that they may see.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetaphysicsIX_1050a_5%E2%80%9310-67)

In summary, the matter used to make a house has potentiality to be a house and both the activity of building and the form of the final house are actualities, which is also a [final cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_cause "Final cause") or end. Then Aristotle proceeds and concludes that the actuality is prior to potentiality in formula, in time and in substantiality. With this definition of the particular substance (i.e., matter and form), Aristotle tries to solve the problem of the unity of the beings, for example, "what is it that makes a man one"? Since, according to [Plato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plato "Plato") there are two Ideas: animal and biped, how then is man a unity? However, according to Aristotle, the potential being (matter) and the actual one (form) are one and the same.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECohen2000-63)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMetaphysicsVIII_1045a%E2%80%93b-68)

### Epistemology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Epistemology")]

Aristotle's immanent realism means his [epistemology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epistemology "Epistemology") is based on the study of things that exist or happen in the world, and rises to knowledge of the universal, whereas for Plato epistemology begins with knowledge of universal [Forms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_Forms "Theory of Forms") (or ideas) and descends to knowledge of particular imitations of these.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2017-57) Aristotle uses [induction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inductive_reasoning "Inductive reasoning") from examples alongside [deduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductive_reasoning "Deductive reasoning"), whereas Plato relies on deduction from *[a priori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A_priori_and_a_posteriori "A priori and a posteriori")* principles.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith2017-57)

## Natural philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Natural philosophy")]

Aristotle's "natural philosophy" spans a wide range of natural phenomena including those now covered by physics, biology and other natural sciences.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWildberg2016-69) In Aristotle's terminology, "natural philosophy" is a branch of philosophy examining the phenomena of the natural world, and includes fields that would be regarded today as physics, biology and other natural sciences. Aristotle's work encompassed virtually all facets of intellectual inquiry. Aristotle makes philosophy in the broad sense coextensive with reasoning, which he also would describe as "science". However, his use of the term *science* carries a different meaning than that covered by the term "scientific method". For Aristotle, "all science (*dianoia*) is either practical, poetical or theoretical" (*Metaphysics* 1025b25). His practical science includes ethics and politics; his poetical science means the study of fine arts including poetry; his theoretical science covers physics, mathematics and metaphysics.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWildberg2016-69)

### Physics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Physics")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Four_Classical_Elements_in_Burning_Log.svg/220px-Four_Classical_Elements_in_Burning_Log.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Four_Classical_Elements_in_Burning_Log.svg)

The four [classical elements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_element "Classical element") (fire, air, water, earth) of [Empedocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empedocles "Empedocles") and Aristotle illustrated with a burning log. The log releases all four elements as it is destroyed.

Main article: [Aristotelian physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_physics "Aristotelian physics")

#### Five elements

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=12 "Edit section: Five elements")]

Main article: [Classical element](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_element "Classical element")

In his *[On Generation and Corruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_Generation_and_Corruption "On Generation and Corruption")*, Aristotle related each of the four elements proposed earlier by [Empedocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empedocles "Empedocles"), [earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_\(classical_element\) "Earth (classical element)"), [water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_\(classical_element\) "Water (classical element)"), [air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_\(classical_element\) "Air (classical element)"), and [fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_\(classical_element\) "Fire (classical element)"), to two of the four sensible qualities, hot, cold, wet, and dry. In the Empedoclean scheme, all matter was made of the four elements, in differing proportions. Aristotle's scheme added the heavenly [aether](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aether_\(classical_element\) "Aether (classical element)"), the divine substance of the [heavenly spheres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celestial_spheres "Celestial spheres"), stars and planets.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd1968133%E2%80%93139,_166%E2%80%93169-70)

| Element                                                                                               | Hot/Cold           | Wet/Dry | Motion                | Modern [state of matter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_matter "State of matter") |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------ | ------- | --------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **[Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth_\(classical_element\) "Earth (classical element)")**    | Cold               | Dry     | Down                  | [Solid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solid "Solid")                                      |
| **[Water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_\(classical_element\) "Water (classical element)")**    | Cold               | Wet     | Down                  | [Liquid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liquid "Liquid")                                   |
| **[Air](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_\(classical_element\) "Air (classical element)")**          | Hot                | Wet     | Up                    | [Gas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas "Gas")                                            |
| **[Fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_\(classical_element\) "Fire (classical element)")**       | Hot                | Dry     | Up                    | [Plasma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasma_\(physics\) "Plasma (physics)")             |
| **[Aether](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aether_\(classical_element\) "Aether (classical element)")** | (divine substance) | —       | Circular (in heavens) | [Vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum "Vacuum")                                   |

#### Motion

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Motion")]

Further information: [History of classical mechanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_classical_mechanics "History of classical mechanics")

Aristotle describes two kinds of motion: "violent" or "unnatural motion", such as that of a thrown stone, in the *Physics* (254b10), and "natural motion", such as of a falling object, in *On the Heavens* (300a20). In violent motion, as soon as the agent stops causing it, the motion stops also: in other words, the natural state of an object is to be at rest,[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllain2016-71)[\[I\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72) since Aristotle does not address [friction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friction "Friction").[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73) With this understanding, it can be observed that, as Aristotle stated, heavy objects (on the ground, say) require more force to make them move; and objects pushed with greater force move faster.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESusskind2011-74)[\[J\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-75) This would imply the equation[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESusskind2011-74)

- - ![{\displaystyle F=mv}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/11033403605ececd49fde3156e3914be4ac47f69),

incorrect in modern physics.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESusskind2011-74)

Natural motion depends on the element concerned: the aether naturally moves in a circle around the heavens,[\[K\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77) while the 4 Empedoclean elements move vertically up (like fire, as is observed) or down (like earth) towards their natural resting places.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73)[\[L\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Aristotle%27s_laws_of_motion.svg/330px-Aristotle%27s_laws_of_motion.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotle%27s_laws_of_motion.svg)

Aristotle's laws of motion. In *[Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physics_\(Aristotle\) "Physics (Aristotle)")* he states that objects fall at a speed proportional to their weight and inversely proportional to the density of the fluid they are immersed in.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73) This is a correct approximation for objects in Earth's gravitational field moving in air or water.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)

In the *Physics* (215a25), Aristotle effectively states a quantitative law, that the speed, v, of a falling body is proportional (say, with constant c) to its weight, W, and inversely proportional to the density,[\[M\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79) ρ, of the fluid in which it is falling:;[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73)

- - ![{\displaystyle v=c{\frac {W}{\rho }}}](https://wikimedia.org/api/rest_v1/media/math/render/svg/63f55362fcfc38cda0b379cbc8a5df0c2c6367b3)

Aristotle implies that in a [vacuum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vacuum "Vacuum") the speed of fall would become infinite, and concludes from this apparent absurdity that a vacuum is not possible.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73) Opinions have varied on whether Aristotle intended to state quantitative laws. Henri Carteron held the "extreme view"[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73) that Aristotle's concept of force was basically qualitative,[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarteron19231%E2%80%9332_and_passim-80) but other authors reject this.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73)

[Archimedes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archimedes "Archimedes") corrected Aristotle's theory that bodies move towards their natural resting places; metal boats can float if they [displace enough water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archimedes%27_principle "Archimedes' principle"); floating depends in Archimedes' scheme on the mass and volume of the object, not, as Aristotle thought, its elementary composition.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)

Aristotle's writings on motion remained influential until the [Early Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern "Early Modern") period. [John Philoponus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Philoponus "John Philoponus") (in [Late antiquity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_antiquity "Late antiquity")) and [Galileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei "Galileo Galilei") (in [Early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period "Early modern period")) are said to have shown by experiment that Aristotle's claim that a heavier object falls faster than a lighter object is incorrect.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWildberg2016-69) A contrary opinion is given by [Carlo Rovelli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlo_Rovelli "Carlo Rovelli"), who argues that Aristotle's physics of motion is correct within its domain of validity, that of objects in the [Earth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earth "Earth")'s gravitational field immersed in a fluid such as air. In this system, heavy bodies in steady fall indeed travel faster than light ones (whether friction is ignored, or not[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)), and they do fall more slowly in a denser medium.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESusskind2011-74)[\[N\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81)

Newton's "forced" motion corresponds to Aristotle's "violent" motion with its external agent, but Aristotle's assumption that the agent's effect stops immediately it stops acting (e.g., the ball leaves the thrower's hand) has awkward consequences: he has to suppose that surrounding fluid helps to push the ball along to make it continue to rise even though the hand is no longer acting on it, resulting in the Medieval [theory of impetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_impetus "Theory of impetus").[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)

#### Four causes

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Four causes")]

Main article: [Four causes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_causes "Four causes")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/77/Aristotle%27s_Four_Causes_of_a_Table.svg/330px-Aristotle%27s_Four_Causes_of_a_Table.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotle%27s_Four_Causes_of_a_Table.svg)

Aristotle argued by analogy with woodwork that a thing takes its form from [four causes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_causes "Four causes"): in the case of a table, the wood used ([material cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Material_cause "Material cause")), its design ([formal cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_cause "Formal cause")), the tools and techniques used ([efficient cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_cause "Efficient cause")), and its decorative or practical purpose ([final cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_cause "Final cause")).[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201588%E2%80%9390-82)

Aristotle suggested that the reason for anything coming about can be attributed to four different types of simultaneously active factors. His term *aitia* is traditionally translated as "cause", but it does not always refer to temporal sequence; it might be better translated as "explanation", but the traditional rendering will be employed here.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd199696%E2%80%93100,_106%E2%80%93107-83)[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHankinson1998159-84)

- [Material cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Material_cause "Material cause") describes the material out of which something is composed. Thus the material cause of a table is wood. It is not about action. It does not mean that one domino knocks over another domino.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd199696%E2%80%93100,_106%E2%80%93107-83)
- The [formal cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_cause "Formal cause") is its form, i.e., the arrangement of that matter. It tells one what a thing is, that a thing is determined by the definition, form, pattern, essence, whole, synthesis or archetype. It embraces the account of causes in terms of fundamental principles or general laws, as the whole (i.e., macrostructure) is the cause of its parts, a relationship known as the whole-part causation. Plainly put, the formal cause is the idea in the mind of the sculptor that brings the sculpture into being. A simple example of the formal cause is the mental image or idea that allows an artist, architect, or engineer to create a drawing.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd199696%E2%80%93100,_106%E2%80%93107-83)
- The [efficient cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efficient_cause "Efficient cause") is "the primary source", or that from which the change under consideration proceeds. It identifies 'what makes of what is made and what causes change of what is changed' and so suggests all sorts of agents, non-living or living, acting as the sources of change or movement or rest. Representing the current understanding of causality as the relation of cause and effect, this covers the modern definitions of "cause" as either the agent or agency or particular events or states of affairs. In the case of two dominoes, when the first is knocked over it causes the second also to fall over.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd199696%E2%80%93100,_106%E2%80%93107-83) In the case of animals, this agency is a combination of [how it develops from the egg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developmental_biology "Developmental biology"), and [how its body functions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physiology "Physiology").[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201591%E2%80%9392,_369%E2%80%93373-85)
- The [final cause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Final_cause "Final cause") (*telos*) is its purpose, the reason why a thing exists or is done, including both purposeful and instrumental actions and activities. The final cause is the purpose or function that something is supposed to serve. This covers modern ideas of motivating causes, such as volition.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELloyd199696%E2%80%93100,_106%E2%80%93107-83) In the case of living things, it implies [adaptation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adaptation "Adaptation") to a particular way of life.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201591%E2%80%9392,_369%E2%80%93373-85)

#### Optics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Optics")]

Further information: [History of optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_optics "History of optics")

Aristotle describes experiments in [optics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optics "Optics") using a [camera obscura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camera_obscura "Camera obscura") in *[Problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Problems_\(Aristotle\) "Problems (Aristotle)")*, book 15. The apparatus consisted of a dark chamber with a small [aperture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aperture "Aperture") that let light in. With it, he saw that whatever shape he made the hole, the sun's image always remained circular. He also noted that increasing the distance between the aperture and the image surface magnified the image.[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELahanas-86)

#### Chance and spontaneity

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Chance and spontaneity")]

Further information: [Accident (philosophy)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_\(philosophy\) "Accident (philosophy)")

According to Aristotle, spontaneity and chance are causes of some things, distinguishable from other types of cause such as simple necessity. Chance as an incidental cause lies in the realm of [accidental things](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accident_\(philosophy\) "Accident (philosophy)"), "from what is spontaneous". There is also more a specific kind of chance, which Aristotle names "luck", that only applies to people's moral choices.[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhysics2.6-87)[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMiller1973204%E2%80%93213-88)

### Astronomy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Astronomy")]

Further information: [History of astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_astronomy "History of astronomy")

In [astronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astronomy "Astronomy"), Aristotle refuted [Democritus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democritus "Democritus")'s claim that the [Milky Way](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milky_Way "Milky Way") was made up of "those stars which are shaded by the earth from the sun's rays," pointing out partly correctly that if "the size of the sun is greater than that of the earth and the distance of the stars from the earth many times greater than that of the sun, then... the sun shines on all the stars and the earth screens none of them."[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeteorology1._8-89) He also wrote descriptions of comets, including the [Great Comet of 371 BC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Comet_of_371_BC "Great Comet of 371 BC").[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeteorology-90)

### Geology and natural sciences

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Geology and natural sciences")]

Further information: [History of geology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_geology "History of geology")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e0/Aerial_image_of_Stromboli_%28view_from_the_northeast%29.jpg/220px-Aerial_image_of_Stromboli_%28view_from_the_northeast%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aerial_image_of_Stromboli_\(view_from_the_northeast\).jpg)

Aristotle noted that the ground level of the [Aeolian islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeolian_islands "Aeolian islands") changed before a [volcanic eruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_eruption "Volcanic eruption").

Aristotle was one of the first people to record any [geological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geology "Geology") observations. He stated that [geological change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uniformitarianism "Uniformitarianism") was too slow to be observed in one person's lifetime.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMoore195613-91)[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMeteorologyBook_1,_Part_14-92) The geologist [Charles Lyell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Lyell "Charles Lyell") noted that Aristotle described such change, including "lakes that had dried up" and "deserts that had become watered by rivers", giving as examples the growth of the [Nile delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_delta "Nile delta") since the time of [Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer "Homer"), and "the upheaving of one of the [Aeolian islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aeolian_islands "Aeolian islands"), previous to a [volcanic eruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_eruption "Volcanic eruption")."'[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELyell183217-93)

*Meteorologica* lends its name to the modern study of meteorology, but its modern usage diverges from the content of Aristotle's ancient treatise on [meteors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteors "Meteors"). The ancient Greeks did use the term for a range of atmospheric phenomena, but also for [earthquakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Earthquakes "Earthquakes") and volcanic eruptions. Aristotle proposed that the cause of earthquakes was a gas or vapor (*anathymiaseis*) that was trapped inside the earth and trying to escape, following other Greek authors [Anaxagoras](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaxagoras "Anaxagoras"), [Empedocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empedocles "Empedocles") and [Democritus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democritus "Democritus").[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94)

Aristotle also made many observations about the hydrologic cycle. For example, he made some of the earliest observations about desalination: he observed early – and correctly – that when seawater is heated, freshwater evaporates and that the oceans are then replenished by the cycle of rainfall and river runoff ("I have proved by experiment that salt water evaporated forms fresh and the vapor does not when it condenses condense into sea water again.")[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-95)

### Biology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=19 "Edit section: Biology")]

Main article: [Aristotle's biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle%27s_biology "Aristotle's biology")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Tremoctopus_violaceus5.jpg/170px-Tremoctopus_violaceus5.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tremoctopus_violaceus5.jpg)

Among many pioneering zoological observations, Aristotle described the reproductive [hectocotyl arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hectocotylus "Hectocotylus") of the [octopus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octopus "Octopus") (bottom left).

#### Empirical research

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Empirical research")]

Aristotle was the first person to study biology systematically,[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi20157-96) and biology forms a large part of his writings. He spent two years observing and describing the zoology of [Lesbos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesbos "Lesbos") and the surrounding seas, including in particular the Pyrrha lagoon in the centre of Lesbos.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201514-97)[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEThompson1910Prefatory_Note-98) His data in *[History of Animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Animals "History of Animals")*, *[Generation of Animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_of_Animals "Generation of Animals")*, *[Movement of Animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Movement_of_Animals "Movement of Animals")*, and *[Parts of Animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parts_of_Animals "Parts of Animals")* are assembled from his own observations,[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99) statements given by people with specialized knowledge, such as beekeepers and fishermen, and less accurate accounts provided by travellers from overseas.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015196,_248-100) His apparent emphasis on animals rather than plants is a historical accident: his works on [botany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botany "Botany") have been lost, but two books on plants by his pupil Theophrastus have survived.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDay20135805%E2%80%935816-101)

Aristotle reports on the sea-life visible from observation on Lesbos and the catches of fishermen. He describes the [catfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catfish "Catfish"), [electric ray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_ray "Electric ray"), and [frogfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frogfish "Frogfish") in detail, as well as [cephalopods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cephalopod "Cephalopod") such as the [octopus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Octopus "Octopus") and [paper nautilus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paper_nautilus "Paper nautilus"). His description of the [hectocotyl arm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hectocotylus "Hectocotylus") of cephalopods, used in sexual reproduction, was widely disbelieved until the 19th century.[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201566%E2%80%9374,_137-102) He gives accurate descriptions of the four-chambered fore-stomachs of [ruminants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruminant "Ruminant"),[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015118%E2%80%93119-103) and of the [ovoviviparous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ovoviviparity "Ovoviviparity") embryological development of the [hound shark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hound_shark "Hound shark").[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201573-104)

He notes that an animal's structure is well matched to function so birds like the [heron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heron "Heron") (which live in marshes with soft mud and live by catching fish) have a long neck, long legs, and a sharp spear-like beak, whereas [ducks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duck "Duck") that swim have short legs and webbed feet.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015135%E2%80%93136-105) [Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin "Charles Darwin"), too, noted these sorts of differences between similar kinds of animal, but unlike Aristotle used the data to come to the theory of [evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evolution "Evolution").[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015206-106) Aristotle's writings can seem to modern readers close to implying evolution, but while Aristotle was aware that new mutations or [hybridizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybridisation_\(biology\) "Hybridisation (biology)") could occur, he saw these as rare accidents. For Aristotle, accidents, like heat waves in winter, must be considered distinct from natural causes. He was thus critical of Empedocles's materialist theory of a "survival of the fittest" origin of living things and their organs, and ridiculed the idea that accidents could lead to orderly results.[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESedley2007189-107) To put his views into modern terms, he nowhere says that different species can have a [common ancestor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_descent "Common descent"), or that one kind can [change into another](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speciation "Speciation"), or that kinds can become [extinct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extinction "Extinction").[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015273-108)

#### Scientific style

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Scientific style")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Two_of_Aristotle%27s_Growth_Laws.svg/310px-Two_of_Aristotle%27s_Growth_Laws.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Two_of_Aristotle%27s_Growth_Laws.svg)

Aristotle inferred growth laws from his observations on animals, including that [brood size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brood_size "Brood size") decreases with body mass, whereas [gestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestation "Gestation") period increases. He was correct in these predictions, at least for mammals: data are shown for mouse and elephant.

Aristotle did not do experiments in the modern sense.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor192242-109) He used the ancient Greek term *pepeiramenoi* to mean observations, or at most investigative procedures like dissection.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015361%E2%80%93365-110) In *Generation of Animals*, he finds a fertilized hen's egg of a suitable stage and opens it to see the embryo's heart beating inside.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2011-111)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015197%E2%80%93200-112)

Instead, he practiced a different style of science: systematically gathering data, discovering patterns common to whole groups of animals, and inferring possible causal explanations from these.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015365%E2%80%93368-113)[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor192249-114) This style is common in modern biology when large amounts of data become available in a new field, such as [genomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genomics "Genomics"). It does not result in the same certainty as experimental science, but it sets out testable hypotheses and constructs a narrative explanation of what is observed. In this sense, [Aristotle's biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle%27s_biology "Aristotle's biology") is scientific.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015365%E2%80%93368-113)

From the data he collected and documented, Aristotle inferred quite a number of [rules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_rules "Biological rules") relating the life-history features of the live-bearing tetrapods (terrestrial placental mammals) that he studied. Among these correct predictions are the following. Brood size decreases with (adult) body mass, so that an elephant has fewer young (usually just one) per brood than a mouse. [Lifespan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy "Life expectancy") increases with [gestation period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestation_period "Gestation period"), and also with body mass, so that elephants live longer than mice, have a longer period of gestation, and are heavier. As a final example, [fecundity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fecundity "Fecundity") decreases with lifespan, so long-lived kinds like elephants have fewer young in total than short-lived kinds like mice.[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015408-115)

#### Classification of living things

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Classification of living things")]

Further information: [Scala naturae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scala_naturae "Scala naturae")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/FMIB_46109_Foetal_Picked_Dogfish%2C_with_the_ovum_attached_%28no_label%29.jpeg/220px-FMIB_46109_Foetal_Picked_Dogfish%2C_with_the_ovum_attached_%28no_label%29.jpeg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:FMIB_46109_Foetal_Picked_Dogfish,_with_the_ovum_attached_\(no_label\).jpeg)

Aristotle recorded that the [embryo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Embryo "Embryo") (fetus pictured) of [a dogfish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mustelus_canis "Mustelus canis") was attached by a cord to a kind of placenta (the [yolk sac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yolk_sac "Yolk sac")), like a higher animal; this formed an exception to the linear scale from highest to lowest.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi201572%E2%80%9374-116)

Aristotle distinguished about 500 species of [animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal "Animal"),[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBergstromDugatkin201235-117)[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhodes19747-118) arranging these in the *History of Animals* in a graded scale of perfection, a nonreligious version of the *[scala naturae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scala_naturae "Scala naturae")*, with man at the top. His system had eleven grades of animal, from highest potential to lowest, expressed in their form at birth: the highest gave [live birth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viviparity "Viviparity") to hot and wet creatures, the lowest laid cold, dry mineral-like eggs. Animals came above [plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant "Plant"), and these in turn were above minerals.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayr1982201%E2%80%93202-119)[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELovejoy1976-120) He grouped what the modern zoologist would call [vertebrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vertebrate "Vertebrate") as the hotter "animals with blood", and below them the colder [invertebrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invertebrate "Invertebrate") as "animals without blood". Those with blood were divided into the live-bearing ([mammals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammal "Mammal")), and the egg-laying ([birds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird "Bird"), [reptiles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reptile "Reptile"), [fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish "Fish")). Those without blood were insects, crustacea (non-shelled – cephalopods, and [shelled](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crustacea "Crustacea")) and the hard-shelled [molluscs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mollusc "Mollusc") ([bivalves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bivalve "Bivalve") and [gastropods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gastropod "Gastropod")). He recognised that animals did not exactly fit into a linear scale, and noted various exceptions, such as that sharks had a [placenta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Placenta "Placenta") like the tetrapods. To a modern biologist, the explanation, not available to Aristotle, is [convergent evolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convergent_evolution "Convergent evolution").[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015111%E2%80%93119-121) Philosophers of science have generally concluded that Aristotle was not interested in taxonomy,[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-122)[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-123) but zoologists who studied this question in the early 21st century think otherwise.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Voultsiadou_et_al._2017-124)[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-125)[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Laurin_2023_Advent-126) He believed that purposive final causes guided all natural processes; this [teleological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teleological "Teleological") view justified his observed data as an expression of formal design.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason197943%E2%80%9344-127)
