### Psychology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=23 "Edit section: Psychology")]

#### Soul

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=24 "Edit section: Soul")]

Further information: [On the Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Soul "On the Soul")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/0a/Aristotelian_Soul.png/330px-Aristotelian_Soul.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotelian_Soul.png)

Aristotle proposed a three-part [structure for souls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul#Aristotle "Soul") of plants, animals, and humans, making humans unique in having all three types of soul.

Aristotle's [psychology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology "Psychology"), given in his treatise *[On the Soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Soul "On the Soul")* (*peri psychēs*), posits three kinds of [soul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soul "Soul") ("psyches"): the vegetative soul, the sensitive soul, and the rational soul. Humans have all three. The vegetative soul is concerned with growth and nourishment. The sensitive soul experiences sensations and movement. The unique part of the human, rational soul is its ability to receive forms of other things and to compare them using the *[nous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nous#Aristotle "Nous")* (intellect) and *logos* (reason).[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015156%E2%80%93163-128)

For Aristotle, the soul is the [form](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hylomorphism#Body%E2%80%93soul_hylomorphism "Hylomorphism") of a living being. Because all beings are composites of form and matter, the form of living beings is that which endows them with what is specific to living beings, e.g. the ability to initiate movement (or in the case of plants, growth and transformations, which Aristotle considers types of movement).[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2016-129) In contrast to earlier philosophers, but in accordance with the Egyptians, he placed the rational soul in the heart, rather than the brain.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason197945-130) Notable is Aristotle's division of sensation and thought, which generally differed from the concepts of previous philosophers, with the exception of [Alcmaeon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcmaeon_of_Croton "Alcmaeon of Croton").[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuthrie2010348-131)

In *On the Soul*, Aristotle famously criticizes Plato's theory of the soul and develops his own in response. The first criticism is against Plato's view of the soul in the *Timaeus* that the soul takes up space and is able to come into physical contact with bodies.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-132) 20th-century scholarship overwhelmingly opposed Aristotle's interpretation of Plato and maintained that he had misunderstood him.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-133) Today's scholars have tended to re-assess Aristotle's interpretation and been more positive about it.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-134) Aristotle's other criticism is that Plato's view of reincarnation entails that it is possible for a soul and its body to be mis-matched; in principle, Aristotle alleges, any soul can go with any body, according to Plato's theory.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135) Aristotle's claim that the soul is the form of a living being eliminates that possibility and thus rules out reincarnation.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-136)

#### Memory

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Memory")]

According to Aristotle in *On the Soul*, memory is the ability to hold a perceived experience in the mind and to distinguish between the internal "appearance" and an occurrence in the past.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloch200712-137) In other words, a memory is a mental picture ([phantasm](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/phantasm "wikt:phantasm")) that can be recovered. Aristotle believed an impression is left on a semi-fluid bodily organ that undergoes several changes in order to make a memory. A memory occurs when [stimuli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stimulus_\(psychology\) "Stimulus (psychology)") such as sights or sounds are so complex that the nervous system cannot receive all the impressions at once. These changes are the same as those involved in the operations of sensation, Aristotelian '[common sense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_sense "Common sense")', and thinking.[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloch200761-138)[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarruthers200716-139)

Aristotle uses the term 'memory' for the actual retaining of an experience in the impression that can develop from sensation, and for the intellectual anxiety that comes with the impression because it is formed at a particular time and processing specific contents. Memory is of the past, prediction is of the future, and sensation is of the present. Retrieval of impressions cannot be performed suddenly. A transitional channel is needed and located in past experiences, both for previous experience and present experience.[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBloch200725-140)

Because Aristotle believes people receive all kinds of sense perceptions and perceive them as impressions, people are continually weaving together new impressions of experiences. To search for these impressions, people search the memory itself.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarren192130-141) Within the memory, if one experience is offered instead of a specific memory, that person will reject this experience until they find what they are looking for. Recollection occurs when one retrieved experience naturally follows another. If the chain of "images" is needed, one memory will stimulate the next. When people recall experiences, they stimulate certain previous experiences until they reach the one that is needed.[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarren192125-142) Recollection is thus the self-directed activity of retrieving the information stored in a memory impression.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTECarruthers200719-143) Only humans can remember impressions of intellectual activity, such as numbers and words. Animals that have perception of time can retrieve memories of their past observations. Remembering involves only perception of the things remembered and of the time passed.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarren1921296-144)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ec/Aristotle_Senses_Perception_Memory_Dreams_Action.svg/500px-Aristotle_Senses_Perception_Memory_Dreams_Action.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotle_Senses_Perception_Memory_Dreams_Action.svg)

Senses, perception, memory, dreams, action in Aristotle's psychology. Impressions are stored in the [sensorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensorium "Sensorium") (the heart), linked by his [laws of association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_association "Laws of association") (similarity, contrast, and [contiguity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contiguity_\(psychology\) "Contiguity (psychology)")).

Aristotle believed the chain of thought, which ends in recollection of certain impressions, was connected systematically in relationships such as similarity, contrast, and [contiguity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contiguity_\(psychology\) "Contiguity (psychology)"), described in his [laws of association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laws_of_association "Laws of association"). Aristotle believed that past experiences are hidden within the mind. A force operates to awaken the hidden material to bring up the actual experience. According to Aristotle, association is the power innate in a mental state, which operates upon the unexpressed remains of former experiences, allowing them to rise and be recalled.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWarren1921259-145)[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESorabji200654-146)

#### Dreams

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Dreams")]

Further information: [Dream § Other](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dream#Other "Dream")

Aristotle describes sleep in *On Sleep and Wakefulness*.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%93423-147) Sleep takes place as a result of overuse of the senses[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShute1941115%E2%80%93118-148) or of digestion,[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%9323-149) so it is vital to the body.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShute1941115%E2%80%93118-148) While a person is asleep, the critical activities, which include thinking, sensing, recalling and remembering, do not function as they do during wakefulness. Since a person cannot sense during sleep, they cannot have desire, which is the result of sensation. However, the senses are able to work during sleep,[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShute1941115%E2%80%9318-150) albeit differently,[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%93423-147) unless they are weary.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShute1941115%E2%80%93118-148)

Dreams do not involve actually sensing a stimulus. In dreams, sensation is still involved, but in an altered manner.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShute1941115%E2%80%93118-148) Aristotle explains that when a person stares at a moving stimulus such as the waves in a body of water, and then looks away, the next thing they look at appears to have a wavelike motion. When a person perceives a stimulus and the stimulus is no longer the focus of their attention, it leaves an impression.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%93423-147) When the body is awake and the senses are functioning properly, a person constantly encounters new stimuli to sense and so the impressions of previously perceived stimuli are ignored.[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShute1941115%E2%80%93118-148) However, during sleep the impressions made throughout the day are noticed as there are no new distracting sensory experiences.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%93423-147) So, dreams result from these lasting impressions. Since impressions are all that are left and not the exact stimuli, dreams do not resemble the actual waking experience.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEModrak2009169%E2%80%93181-151) During sleep, a person is in an altered state of mind. Aristotle compares a sleeping person to a person who is overtaken by strong feelings toward a stimulus. For example, a person who has a strong infatuation with someone may begin to think they see that person everywhere because they are so overtaken by their feelings. Since a person sleeping is in a suggestible state and unable to make judgements, they become easily deceived by what appears in their dreams, like the infatuated person.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%93423-147) This leads the person to believe the dream is real, even when the dreams are absurd in nature.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolowchak1996405%E2%80%93423-147) In *De Anima* iii 3, Aristotle ascribes the ability to create, to store, and to recall images in the absence of perception to the faculty of imagination, *phantasia*.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEShields2016-129)

One component of Aristotle's theory of dreams disagrees with previously held beliefs. He claimed that dreams are not foretelling and not sent by a divine being. Aristotle reasoned naturalistically that instances in which dreams do resemble future events are simply coincidences.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWebb1990174%E2%80%93184-152) Aristotle claimed that a dream is first established by the fact that the person is asleep when they experience it. If a person had an image appear for a moment after waking up or if they see something in the dark it is not considered a dream because they were awake when it occurred. Secondly, any sensory experience that is perceived while a person is asleep does not qualify as part of a dream. For example, if, while a person is sleeping, a door shuts and in their dream they hear a door is shut, this sensory experience is not part of the dream. Lastly, the images of dreams must be a result of lasting impressions of waking sensory experiences.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEModrak2009169%E2%80%93181-151)

## Practical philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Practical philosophy")]

Aristotle's practical philosophy covers areas such as [ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethics "Ethics"), [politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics "Politics"), [economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics "Economics"), and [rhetoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetoric "Rhetoric").[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWildberg2016-69)

| Too little      | Virtuous mean   | Too much       |
| --------------- | --------------- | -------------- |
| Humbleness      | High-mindedness | Vainglory      |
| Lack of purpose | Right ambition  | Over-ambition  |
| Spiritlessness  | Good temper     | Irascibility   |
| Rudeness        | Civility        | Obsequiousness |
| Cowardice       | Courage         | Rashness       |
| Insensibility   | Self-control    | Intemperance   |
| Sarcasm         | Sincerity       | Boastfulness   |
| Boorishness     | Wit             | Buffoonery     |
| Callousness     | Just resentment | Spitefulness   |
| Pettiness       | Generosity      | Vulgarity      |
| Meanness        | Liberality      | Wastefulness   |

### Ethics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Ethics")]

Main article: [Aristotelian ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelian_ethics "Aristotelian ethics")

Aristotle considered ethics to be a practical rather than theoretical study, i.e., one aimed at becoming good and doing good rather than knowing for its own sake. He wrote several treatises on ethics, most notably including the *[Nicomachean Ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachean_Ethics "Nicomachean Ethics")*.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKraut2001-153)

Aristotle taught that virtue has to do with the proper function (*ergon*) of a thing. An eye is only a good eye in so much as it can see because the proper function of an eye is sight. Aristotle reasoned that humans must have a function specific to humans, and that this function must be an activity of the *[psuchē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Anima "De Anima")* (*soul*) in accordance with reason (*[logos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logos "Logos")*). Aristotle identified such an optimum activity (the virtuous mean, between the accompanying vices of excess or deficiency[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHumphreys2009-40)) of the soul as the aim of all human deliberate action, *[eudaimonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eudaimonia "Eudaimonia")*, generally translated as "happiness" or sometimes "well-being". To have the potential of ever being happy in this way necessarily requires a good character (*ēthikē* *[aretē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arete_\(moral_virtue\) "Arete (moral virtue)")*), often translated as moral or ethical virtue or excellence.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENicomachean_Ethics-154)

Aristotle taught that to achieve a virtuous and potentially happy character requires a first stage of having the fortune to be habituated not deliberately, but by teachers, and experience, leading to a later stage in which one consciously chooses to do the best things. When the best people come to live life this way their practical wisdom (*[phronesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phronesis "Phronesis")*) and their intellect (*[nous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nous "Nous")*) can develop with each other towards the highest possible human virtue, the wisdom of an accomplished theoretical or speculative thinker, or in other words, a philosopher.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENicomachean_EthicsBook_VI-155)

### Politics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Politics")]

Main article: [Politics (Aristotle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_\(Aristotle\) "Politics (Aristotle)")

In addition to his works on ethics, which address the individual, Aristotle addressed the city in his work titled *[Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_\(Aristotle\) "Politics (Aristotle)")*. Aristotle considered the city to be a natural community. Moreover, he considered the city to be prior in importance to the family, which in turn is prior to the individual, "for the whole must of necessity be prior to the part".[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPolitics1253a19%E2%80%93124-156) He famously stated that "man is by nature a political animal" and argued that humanity's defining factor among others in the animal kingdom is its rationality.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle2009320%E2%80%93321-157) Aristotle conceived of politics as being like an organism rather than like a machine, and as a collection of parts none of which can exist without the others. Aristotle's conception of the city is organic, and he is considered one of the first to conceive of the city in this manner.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEbensteinEbenstein200259-158)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Aristotle%27s_constitutions.svg/330px-Aristotle%27s_constitutions.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotle%27s_constitutions.svg)

Aristotle's classifications of political constitutions.

The common modern understanding of a political community as a modern state is quite different from Aristotle's understanding. Although he was aware of the existence and potential of larger empires, the natural community according to Aristotle was the city (*[polis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polis "Polis")*) which functions as a political "community" or "partnership" (*koinōnia*). The aim of the city is not just to avoid injustice or for economic stability, but rather to allow at least some citizens the possibility to live a good life, and to perform beautiful acts: "The political partnership must be regarded, therefore, as being for the sake of noble actions, not for the sake of living together." This is distinguished from modern approaches, beginning with [social contract](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_contract "Social contract") theory, according to which individuals leave the [state of nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_nature "State of nature") because of "fear of violent death" or its "inconveniences".[\[O\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-159)

In *[Protrepticus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protrepticus_\(Aristotle\) "Protrepticus (Aristotle)")*, the character 'Aristotle' states:[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutchinsonJohnson201522-160)

> For we all agree that the most excellent man should rule, i.e., the supreme by nature, and that the law rules and alone is authoritative; but the law is a kind of intelligence, i.e. a discourse based on intelligence. And again, what standard do we have, what criterion of good things, that is more precise than the intelligent man? For all that this man will choose, if the choice is based on his knowledge, are good things and their contraries are bad. And since everybody chooses most of all what conforms to their own proper dispositions (a just man choosing to live justly, a man with bravery to live bravely, likewise a self-controlled man to live with self-control), it is clear that the intelligent man will choose most of all to be intelligent; for this is the function of that capacity. Hence it's evident that, according to the most authoritative judgment, intelligence is supreme among goods.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutchinsonJohnson201522-160)

As Plato's disciple Aristotle was rather critical concerning democracy and, following the outline of certain ideas from Plato's *[Statesman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statesman_\(dialogue\) "Statesman (dialogue)")*, he developed a coherent theory of integrating various forms of power into a so-called mixed state:

> It is ... constitutional to take ... from oligarchy that offices are to be elected, and from democracy that this is not to be on a property-qualification. This then is the mode of the mixture; and the mark of a good mixture of democracy and oligarchy is when it is possible to speak of the same constitution as a democracy and as an oligarchy.
>
> — Aristotle. *Politics*, Book 4, 1294b.10–18

### Economics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Economics")]

Main article: [Politics (Aristotle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_\(Aristotle\) "Politics (Aristotle)")

Aristotle made substantial contributions to [economic thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economics "Economics"), especially to thought in the Middle Ages.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbins200020%E2%80%9324-161) In *[Politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_\(Aristotle\) "Politics (Aristotle)")*, Aristotle addresses the city, [property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Property "Property"), and [trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade "Trade"). His response to criticisms of [private property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Private_property "Private property"), in [Lionel Robbins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lionel_Robbins "Lionel Robbins")'s view, anticipated later proponents of private property among philosophers and economists, as it related to the overall [utility](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utility "Utility") of social arrangements.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbins200020%E2%80%9324-161) Aristotle believed that although communal arrangements may seem beneficial to society, and that although private property is often blamed for social strife, such evils in fact come from [human nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_nature "Human nature"). In *Politics*, Aristotle offers one of the earliest accounts of the origin of [money](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Money "Money").[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERobbins200020%E2%80%9324-161) Money came into use because people became dependent on one another, importing what they needed and exporting the surplus. For the sake of convenience, people then agreed to deal in something that is intrinsically useful and easily applicable, such as iron or [silver](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Silver "Silver").[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle194816%E2%80%9328-162)

Aristotle's discussions on [retail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retail "Retail") and [interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interest "Interest") was a major influence on economic thought in the Middle Ages. He had a low opinion of retail, believing that contrary to using money to procure things one needs in managing the household, retail trade seeks to make a [profit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Profit_\(economics\) "Profit (economics)"). It thus uses goods as a means to an end, rather than as an end unto itself. He believed that retail trade was in this way unnatural. Similarly, Aristotle considered making a profit through interest unnatural, as it makes a gain out of the money itself, and not from its use.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle194816%E2%80%9328-162)

Aristotle gave a summary of the function of money that was perhaps remarkably precocious for his time. He wrote that because it is impossible to determine the value of every good through a count of the number of other goods it is worth, the necessity arises of a single universal standard of measurement. Money thus allows for the association of different goods and makes them "commensurable".[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle194816%E2%80%9328-162) He goes on to state that money is also useful for future exchange, making it a sort of security. That is, "if we do not want a thing now, we shall be able to get it when we do want it".[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle194816%E2%80%9328-162)

Main article: [Rhetoric (Aristotle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhetoric_\(Aristotle\) "Rhetoric (Aristotle)")

Aristotle's *Rhetoric* proposes that a speaker can use three basic kinds of appeals to persuade his audience: *[ethos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethos "Ethos")* (an appeal to the speaker's character), *[pathos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pathos "Pathos")* (an appeal to the audience's emotion), and *[logos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logos "Logos")* (an appeal to logical reasoning).[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGarver1994109%E2%80%93110-163) He also categorizes rhetoric into three genres: [epideictic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epideictic "Epideictic") (ceremonial speeches dealing with praise or blame), [forensic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forensic_rhetoric "Forensic rhetoric") (judicial speeches over guilt or innocence), and [deliberative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deliberative_rhetoric "Deliberative rhetoric") (speeches calling on an audience to decide on an issue).[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERorty19963%E2%80%937-164) Aristotle also outlines two kinds of rhetorical [proofs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proof_\(truth\) "Proof (truth)"): *[enthymeme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enthymeme "Enthymeme")* (proof by [syllogism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syllogism "Syllogism")) and *[paradeigma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paradeigma "Paradeigma")* (proof by example).[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrimaldi199871-165)

### Poetics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Poetics")]

Main article: [Poetics (Aristotle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetics_\(Aristotle\) "Poetics (Aristotle)")

Aristotle writes in his *Poetics* that [epic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epic_poetry "Epic poetry"), tragedy, comedy, [dithyrambic poetry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dithyramb "Dithyramb"), painting, sculpture, music, and dance are all fundamentally acts of *[mimesis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mimesis "Mimesis")* ("imitation"), each varying in imitation by medium, object, and manner.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliwell2002152%E2%80%93159-166)[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoeticsI_1447a-167) He applies the term *mimesis* both as a property of a work of art and also as the product of the artist's intention[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliwell2002152%E2%80%93159-166) and contends that the audience's realisation of the *mimesis* is vital to understanding the work itself.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliwell2002152%E2%80%93159-166) Aristotle states that *mimesis* is a natural instinct of humanity that separates humans from animals[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliwell2002152%E2%80%93159-166)[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoeticsIV-168) and that all human artistry "follows the pattern of nature".[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliwell2002152%E2%80%93159-166) Because of this, Aristotle believed that each of the mimetic arts possesses what [Stephen Halliwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Halliwell_\(academic\) "Stephen Halliwell (academic)") calls "highly structured procedures for the achievement of their purposes."[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHalliwell2002152%E2%80%9359-169) For example, music imitates with the media of rhythm and harmony, whereas dance imitates with rhythm alone, and poetry with language. The forms also differ in their object of imitation. Comedy, for instance, is a dramatic imitation of men worse than average; whereas tragedy imitates men slightly better than average. Lastly, the forms differ in their manner of imitation – through narrative or character, through change or no change, and through drama or no drama.[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoeticsIII-170)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/B%C3%A9nigne_Gagneraux%2C_The_Blind_Oedipus_Commending_his_Children_to_the_Gods.jpg/300px-B%C3%A9nigne_Gagneraux%2C_The_Blind_Oedipus_Commending_his_Children_to_the_Gods.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:B%C3%A9nigne_Gagneraux,_The_Blind_Oedipus_Commending_his_Children_to_the_Gods.jpg)

*The Blind Oedipus Commending his Children to the Gods* (1784) by [Bénigne Gagneraux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%A9nigne_Gagneraux "Bénigne Gagneraux"). In his *[Poetics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poetics_\(Aristotle\) "Poetics (Aristotle)")*, Aristotle uses the tragedy *[Oedipus Tyrannus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oedipus_Rex "Oedipus Rex")* by [Sophocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sophocles "Sophocles") as an example of how the perfect tragedy should be structured, with a generally good protagonist who starts the play prosperous, but loses everything through some *[hamartia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamartia "Hamartia")* (fault).[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKaufmann196856%E2%80%9360-171)

While it is believed that Aristotle's *Poetics* originally comprised two books – one on comedy and one on tragedy – only the portion that focuses on tragedy has survived. Aristotle taught that tragedy is composed of six elements: plot-structure, character, style, thought, spectacle, and lyric poetry.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoeticsVI-172) The characters in a tragedy are merely a means of driving the story; and the plot, not the characters, is the chief focus of tragedy. Tragedy is the imitation of action arousing pity and fear, and is meant to effect the [catharsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catharsis "Catharsis") of those same emotions. Aristotle concludes *Poetics* with a discussion on which, if either, is superior: epic or tragic mimesis. He suggests that because tragedy possesses all the attributes of an epic, possibly possesses additional attributes such as spectacle and music, is more unified, and achieves the aim of its mimesis in shorter scope, it can be considered superior to epic.[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPoeticsXXVI-173) Aristotle was a keen systematic collector of riddles, folklore, and proverbs; he and his school had a special interest in the riddles of the [Delphic Oracle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pythia "Pythia") and studied the fables of [Aesop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aesop "Aesop").[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAesop1998Introduction,_xi%E2%80%93xii-174)

### Gender and sexuality

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Gender and sexuality")]

Aristotle asserted that men were superior to women writing "the male is by nature superior and the female inferior, the male ruler and the female subject".[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-175)

On this ground, proponents of [feminist metaphysics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feminist_metaphysics "Feminist metaphysics") have accused Aristotle of [misogyny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misogyny "Misogyny")[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFreeland1998-176) and [sexism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexism "Sexism").[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorsink197983%E2%80%93112-177) However, Aristotle gave equal weight to women's happiness as he did to men's, and commented in his *Rhetoric* that the things that lead to happiness need to be in women as well as men.[\[P\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-179) Aristotle, like other Greek philosophers such Plato and [Xenophon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophon "Xenophon"), rejected [homosexuality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homosexuality "Homosexuality"), believing that it could lead people to become immoral due to the fact that he believed it went against nature's purpose.[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-180)[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-181)

## Transmission

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=34 "Edit section: Transmission")]

Further information: [List of writers influenced by Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_writers_influenced_by_Aristotle "List of writers influenced by Aristotle")

More than 2300 years after his death, Aristotle remains one of the most influential people who ever lived.[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi20158-182)[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle's_Influence2018-183)[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-184) He contributed to almost every field of human knowledge then in existence, and he was the founder of many new fields. According to the philosopher [Bryan Magee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bryan_Magee "Bryan Magee"), "it is doubtful whether any human being has ever known as much as he did".[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagee201034-185) Aristotle has been regarded as the first [scientist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientist "Scientist").[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-186)[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-187)

Among countless other achievements, Aristotle was the founder of [formal logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formal_logic "Formal logic"),[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGuthrie1990156-188) pioneered the study of [zoology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoology "Zoology"), and left every future scientist and philosopher in his debt through his contributions to the scientific method.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle_\(Greek_philosopher\)-45)[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDurant200692-189)[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKukkonen201070%E2%80%9377-190) Taneli Kukkonen, observes that his achievement in founding two sciences is unmatched, and his reach in influencing "every branch of intellectual enterprise" including Western ethical and political theory, theology, rhetoric, and literary analysis is equally long. As a result, Kukkonen argues, any analysis of reality today "will almost certainly carry Aristotelian overtones ... evidence of an exceptionally forceful mind."[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKukkonen201070%E2%80%9377-190) [Jonathan Barnes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Barnes "Jonathan Barnes") wrote that "an account of Aristotle's intellectual afterlife would be little less than a history of European thought".[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes198286-191)

Aristotle has been called the father of logic, biology, political science, zoology, embryology, natural law, scientific method, rhetoric, psychology, realism, criticism, individualism, teleology, and meteorology.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LitList-193)

The scholar Taneli Kukkonen notes that "in the best 20th-century scholarship Aristotle comes alive as a thinker wrestling with the full weight of the Greek philosophical tradition."[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKukkonen201070%E2%80%9377-190) What follows is an overview of the transmission and influence of his texts and ideas into the modern era.

### His successor, Theophrastus

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=35 "Edit section: His successor, Theophrastus")]

Main articles: [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus "Theophrastus") and [Historia Plantarum (Theophrastus)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_Plantarum_\(Theophrastus\) "Historia Plantarum (Theophrastus)")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/161Theophrastus_161_frontespizio.jpg/170px-161Theophrastus_161_frontespizio.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:161Theophrastus_161_frontespizio.jpg)

[Frontispiece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Book_frontispiece "Book frontispiece") to a 1644 version of [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus "Theophrastus")'s *[Historia Plantarum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_Plantarum_\(Theophrastus\) "Historia Plantarum (Theophrastus)")*, originally written around 300 BC.

Aristotle's pupil and successor, [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus "Theophrastus"), wrote the *[History of Plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Historia_Plantarum_\(Theophrastus\) "Historia Plantarum (Theophrastus)")*, a pioneering work in botany. Some of his technical terms remain in use, such as [carpel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carpel "Carpel") from *carpos*, fruit, and [pericarp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pericarp "Pericarp"), from *pericarpion*, seed chamber.[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHooker1831219-194) Theophrastus was much less concerned with formal causes than Aristotle was, instead pragmatically describing how plants functioned.[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayr198290%E2%80%9391-195)[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason197946-196)

### Later Greek philosophy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=36 "Edit section: Later Greek philosophy")]

Further information: [Peripatetic school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripatetic_school "Peripatetic school")

The immediate influence of Aristotle's work was felt as the [Lyceum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum "Lyceum") grew into the [Peripatetic school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripatetic_school "Peripatetic school"). Aristotle's students included [Aristoxenus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristoxenus "Aristoxenus"), [Dicaearchus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dicaearchus "Dicaearchus"), [Demetrius of Phalerum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demetrius_of_Phalerum "Demetrius of Phalerum"), [Eudemos of Rhodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eudemos_of_Rhodes "Eudemos of Rhodes"), [Harpalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harpalus "Harpalus"), [Hephaestion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hephaestion "Hephaestion"), [Mnason of Phocis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mnason_of_Phocis "Mnason of Phocis"), [Nicomachus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachus_\(son_of_Aristotle\) "Nicomachus (son of Aristotle)"), and Theophrastus. Aristotle's influence over [Alexander the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_the_Great "Alexander the Great") is seen in the latter's bringing with him on his expedition a host of zoologists, botanists, and researchers. He had also learned a great deal about [Persian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persian_people "Persian people") customs and traditions from his teacher. Although his respect for Aristotle was diminished as his travels made it clear that much of Aristotle's geography was clearly wrong, when the old philosopher released his works to the public, Alexander complained "Thou hast not done well to publish thy acroamatic doctrines; for in what shall I surpass other men if those doctrines wherein I have been trained are to be all men's common property?"[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPlutarch1919Part_1,_7:7-197)

### Hellenistic science

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=37 "Edit section: Hellenistic science")]

Further information: [Ancient Greek medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek_medicine "Ancient Greek medicine")

After Theophrastus, the Lyceum failed to produce any original work. Though interest in Aristotle's ideas survived, they were generally taken unquestioningly.[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnnas2001252-198) It is not until the age of [Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexandria "Alexandria") under the [Ptolemies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ptolemaic_Kingdom "Ptolemaic Kingdom") that advances in biology can be again found.

The first medical teacher at Alexandria, [Herophilus of Chalcedon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herophilos "Herophilos"), corrected Aristotle, placing intelligence in the brain, and connected the nervous system to motion and sensation. Herophilus also distinguished between [veins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vein "Vein") and [arteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artery "Artery"), noting that the latter [pulse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulse "Pulse") while the former do not.[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMason197956-199) Though a few ancient [atomists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomism "Atomism") such as [Lucretius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucretius "Lucretius") challenged the [teleological](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teleology "Teleology") viewpoint of Aristotelian ideas about life, [teleology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teleology "Teleology") (and after the rise of Christianity, [natural theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_theology "Natural theology")) would remain central to biological thought essentially until the 18th and 19th centuries. [Ernst Mayr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Mayr "Ernst Mayr") states that there was "nothing of any real consequence in biology after Lucretius and [Galen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galen "Galen") until the Renaissance."[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayr198590%E2%80%9394-200)

### Revival

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=38 "Edit section: Revival")]

Following the decline of the Roman Empire, Aristotle's vast philosophical and scientific corpus lay largely dormant in the West. However, his works underwent a remarkable revival in the Abbasid Caliphate.[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-201) Translated into Arabic alongside other Greek classics, Aristotle's logic, ethics, and natural philosophy ignited the minds of early Islamic scholars.[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-202)

Through meticulous commentaries and critical engagements, figures like [Al-Farabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Farabi "Al-Farabi") and [Ibn Sina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna "Avicenna") (Avicenna) breathed new life into Aristotle's ideas. They harmonized his logic with Islamic theology, employed his scientific methodologies to explore the natural world, and even reinterpreted his ethics within the framework of Islamic morality. This revival was not mere imitation. Islamic thinkers embraced Aristotle's rigorous methods while simultaneously challenging his conclusions where they diverged from their own religious beliefs.[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-203)

### Byzantine scholars

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=39 "Edit section: Byzantine scholars")]

See also: [Commentaries on Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commentaries_on_Aristotle "Commentaries on Aristotle") and [Byzantine Aristotelianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Byzantine_Aristotelianism "Byzantine Aristotelianism")

Greek Christian scribes played a crucial role in the preservation of Aristotle by copying all the extant Greek language manuscripts of the corpus. The first Greek Christians to comment extensively on Aristotle were Philoponus, Elias, and David in the sixth century, and [Stephen of Alexandria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_of_Alexandria "Stephen of Alexandria") in the early seventh century.[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESorabji199020,_28,_35%E2%80%9336-204) [John Philoponus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Philoponus "John Philoponus") stands out for having attempted a fundamental critique of Aristotle's views on the eternity of the world, movement, and other elements of Aristotelian thought.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESorabji1990233%E2%80%93724-205) Philoponus questioned Aristotle's teaching of physics, noting its flaws and introducing the [theory of impetus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theory_of_impetus "Theory of impetus") to explain his observations. [\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELindberg1992162-206)

After a hiatus of several centuries, formal commentary by Eustratius and [Michael of Ephesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_of_Ephesus "Michael of Ephesus") reappeared in the late eleventh and early twelfth centuries, apparently sponsored by [Anna Comnena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Comnena "Anna Comnena"). [\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESorabji199020%E2%80%9321,_28%E2%80%9329,_393%E2%80%93406,_407%E2%80%93408-207)

### Medieval Islamic world

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=40 "Edit section: Medieval Islamic world")]

Further information: [Logic in Islamic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logic_in_Islamic_philosophy "Logic in Islamic philosophy") and [Transmission of the Greek Classics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmission_of_the_Greek_Classics "Transmission of the Greek Classics")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/02/Ibn_Bakht%C4%ABsh%C5%AB%CA%BF%2C_Kit%C4%81b_na%CA%BFt_al-%E1%B8%A5ayaw%C4%81n_probably_Baghdad%2C_c._1225._London%2C_British_Library%2C_Or._2784%2C_A_student_sitting_with_Aristotle_%28right%29.jpg/170px-Ibn_Bakht%C4%ABsh%C5%AB%CA%BF%2C_Kit%C4%81b_na%CA%BFt_al-%E1%B8%A5ayaw%C4%81n_probably_Baghdad%2C_c._1225._London%2C_British_Library%2C_Or._2784%2C_A_student_sitting_with_Aristotle_%28right%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ibn_Bakht%C4%ABsh%C5%AB%CA%BF,_Kit%C4%81b_na%CA%BFt_al-%E1%B8%A5ayaw%C4%81n_probably_Baghdad,_c._1225._London,_British_Library,_Or._2784,_A_student_sitting_with_Aristotle_\(right\).jpg)

Islamic portrayal of Aristotle (right) in the *[Kitāb naʿt al-ḥayawān](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kit%C4%81b_na%CA%BFt_al-%E1%B8%A5ayaw%C4%81n "Kitāb naʿt al-ḥayawān")*, c. 1220.[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-208)

Aristotle was one of the most revered thinkers in early [Islamic theology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_theology "Islamic theology"). Most of the still extant works of Aristotle,[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKennedy-Day1998-209) as well as a number of the original Greek commentaries, were translated into Arabic and studied by Muslim philosophers, scientists, and scholars. [Averroes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Averroes "Averroes"), [Avicenna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avicenna "Avicenna"), and [Alpharabius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpharabius "Alpharabius"), who wrote on Aristotle in great depth, also influenced [Thomas Aquinas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_Aquinas "Thomas Aquinas") and other Western Christian scholastic philosophers. [Alkindus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkindus "Alkindus") greatly admired Aristotle's philosophy,[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStaley1989-210) and Averroes spoke of Aristotle as the "exemplar" for all future philosophers. [\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAverroes1953III,_2,_43-211) Medieval Muslim scholars regularly described Aristotle as the "First Teacher".[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKennedy-Day1998-209) The title was later used by Western philosophers (as in the famous poem of [Dante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante_Alighieri "Dante Alighieri")) who were influenced by the tradition of [Islamic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_philosophy "Islamic philosophy"). [\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTENasr199659%E2%80%9360-212)

### Medieval Europe

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=41 "Edit section: Medieval Europe")]

Further information: [Aristotelianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotelianism "Aristotelianism") and [Syllogism § Medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syllogism#Medieval "Syllogism")

With the loss of the study of ancient Greek in the early [medieval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval "Medieval") Latin West, Aristotle was practically unknown there from c. CE 600 to c. 1100 except through the Latin translation of the *Organon* made by [Boethius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boethius "Boethius"). In the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, interest in Aristotle revived and Latin Christians had translations made, both from Arabic translations, such as those by [Gerard of Cremona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_of_Cremona "Gerard of Cremona"),[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHasse2014-213) and from the original Greek, such as those by [James of Venice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_of_Venice "James of Venice") and [William of Moerbeke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_of_Moerbeke "William of Moerbeke").

After the [Scholastic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholasticism "Scholasticism") Thomas Aquinas wrote his *[Summa Theologica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Summa_Theologica "Summa Theologica")*, working from Moerbeke's translations and calling Aristotle "The Philosopher",[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAquinas2013-214) the demand for Aristotle's writings grew, and the [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language "Greek language") manuscripts returned to the West, stimulating a revival of Aristotelianism in Europe that continued into the [Renaissance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renaissance "Renaissance").[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKuhn2018-215) These thinkers blended Aristotelian philosophy with Christianity, bringing the thought of Ancient Greece into the Middle Ages. Scholars such as Boethius, [Peter Abelard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Abelard "Peter Abelard"), and [John Buridan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Buridan "John Buridan") worked on Aristotelian logic.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELagerlund-216)

According to scholar Roger Theodore Lafferty, [Dante](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dante "Dante") built up the philosophy of the *[Comedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Divine_Comedy "Divine Comedy")* with the works of Aristotle as a foundation, just as the scholastics used Aristotle as the basis for their thinking. Dante knew Aristotle directly from Latin translations of his works and indirectly through quotations in the works of [Albert Magnus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Magnus "Albert Magnus").[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-217) Dante even acknowledges Aristotle's influence explicitly in the poem, specifically when Virgil justifies the Inferno's structure by citing the *[Nicomachean Ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachean_Ethics "Nicomachean Ethics")*.[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-218) Dante famously refers to him as "he / Who is acknowledged Master of those who know".[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-219)[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKukkonen201074-220)

### Medieval Judaism

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=42 "Edit section: Medieval Judaism")]

[Moses Maimonides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maimonides "Maimonides") (considered to be the foremost intellectual figure of medieval Judaism)[\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-221) adopted Aristotelianism from the Islamic scholars and based his *[Guide for the Perplexed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guide_for_the_Perplexed "Guide for the Perplexed")* on it and that became the basis of Jewish [scholastic philosophy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scholastic_philosophy "Scholastic philosophy"). Maimonides also considered Aristotle to be the greatest philosopher that ever lived, and styled him as the "chief of the philosophers".[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-222)[\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-223)[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-224) Also, in his letter to [Samuel ibn Tibbon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samuel_ibn_Tibbon "Samuel ibn Tibbon"), Maimonides observes that there is no need for Samuel to study the writings of philosophers who preceded Aristotle because the works of the latter are "sufficient by themselves and \[superior] to all that were written before them. His intellect, Aristotle's is the extreme limit of human intellect, apart from him upon whom the divine emanation has flowed forth to such an extent that they reach the level of prophecy, there being no level higher".[\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-225)

### Early Modern science

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=43 "Edit section: Early Modern science")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6b/William_Harvey_%281578-1657%29_Venenbild.jpg/220px-William_Harvey_%281578-1657%29_Venenbild.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:William_Harvey_\(1578-1657\)_Venenbild.jpg)

[William Harvey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Harvey "William Harvey")'s *[De Motu Cordis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exercitatio_Anatomica_de_Motu_Cordis_et_Sanguinis_in_Animalibus "Exercitatio Anatomica de Motu Cordis et Sanguinis in Animalibus")*, 1628, showed that the [blood circulated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circulation_of_the_blood "Circulation of the blood"), contrary to classical era thinking.

In the [Early Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern "Early Modern") period, scientists such as [William Harvey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Harvey "William Harvey") in England and [Galileo Galilei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_Galilei "Galileo Galilei") in Italy reacted against the theories of Aristotle and other classical era thinkers like [Galen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galen "Galen"), establishing new theories based to some degree on observation and experiment. Harvey demonstrated the [circulation of the blood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circulation_of_the_blood "Circulation of the blood"), establishing that the heart functioned as a pump rather than being the seat of the soul and the controller of the body's heat, as Aristotle thought.[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAird2011118%E2%80%9329-226) Galileo used more doubtful arguments to displace Aristotle's physics, proposing that bodies all fall at the same speed whatever their weight.[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMachamer2017-227)

### 18th and 19th-century science

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=44 "Edit section: 18th and 19th-century science")]

The English mathematician [George Boole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Boole "George Boole") fully accepted Aristotle's logic, but decided "to go under, over, and beyond" it with his system of [algebraic logic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boolean_algebra "Boolean algebra") in his 1854 book *[The Laws of Thought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Laws_of_Thought "The Laws of Thought")*. This gives logic a mathematical foundation with equations, enables it to solve equations as well as check [validity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Validity_\(logic\) "Validity (logic)"), and allows it to handle a wider class of problems by expanding propositions of any number of terms, not just two.[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoole2003-228)

[Charles Darwin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Darwin "Charles Darwin") regarded Aristotle as the most important contributor to the subject of biology. In an 1882 letter he wrote that "Linnaeus and Cuvier have been my two gods, though in very different ways, but they were mere schoolboys to old Aristotle".[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Wilkins_2009-229)[\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pasipoularides_2010-230) Also, in later editions of the book "[On the Origin of Species](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/On_the_Origin_of_Species "On the Origin of Species")', Darwin traced evolutionary ideas as far back as Aristotle;[\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-231) the text he cites is a summary by Aristotle of the ideas of the earlier Greek philosopher [Empedocles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empedocles "Empedocles").[\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-232)

### Present science

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=45 "Edit section: Present science")]

The philosopher [Bertrand Russell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertrand_Russell "Bertrand Russell") claims that "almost every serious intellectual advance has had to begin with an attack on some Aristotelian doctrine". Russell calls Aristotle's ethics "repulsive", and labelled his logic "as definitely antiquated as Ptolemaic astronomy". Russell states that these errors make it difficult to do historical justice to Aristotle, until one remembers what an advance he made upon all of his predecessors.[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERussell1972Chapter_19_%22Aristotle's_Metaphysics%22-233)

The Dutch historian of science [Eduard Jan Dijksterhuis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduard_Jan_Dijksterhuis "Eduard Jan Dijksterhuis") writes that Aristotle and his predecessors showed the difficulty of science by "proceed\[ing] so readily to frame a theory of such a general character" on limited evidence from their senses.[\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDijksterhuis196972-234) In 1985, the biologist [Peter Medawar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Medawar "Peter Medawar") could still state in "pure seventeenth century"[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015353-235) tones that Aristotle had assembled "a strange and generally speaking rather tiresome farrago of hearsay, imperfect observation, wishful thinking and credulity amounting to downright gullibility".[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015353-235)[\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMedawarMedawar198428-236)

Zoologists have frequently mocked Aristotle for errors and unverified secondhand reports. However, modern observation has confirmed several of his more surprising claims.[\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-237)[\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Forbes-238)[\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015137%E2%80%93138-239) Aristotle's work remains largely unknown to modern scientists, though zoologists sometimes mention him as the father of biology[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015352-192) or in particular of [marine biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_biology "Marine biology").[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-MarineBio-240) Practising zoologists are unlikely to adhere to Aristotle's chain of being, but its influence is still perceptible in the use of the terms "lower" and "upper" to designate taxa such as groups of plants.[\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Rigato_&_Minelli_2013-241) The evolutionary biologist [Armand Marie Leroi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armand_Marie_Leroi "Armand Marie Leroi") has reconstructed Aristotle's biology,[\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeroi2015-242) while [Niko Tinbergen's four questions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tinbergen%27s_four_questions "Tinbergen's four questions"), based on Aristotle's four causes, are used to analyse [animal behaviour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_behaviour "Animal behaviour"); they examine [function](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Function_\(biology\) "Function (biology)"), [phylogeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogeny "Phylogeny"), [mechanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanism_\(biology\) "Mechanism (biology)"), and [ontogeny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ontogeny "Ontogeny").[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDougall-Shackleton20112076%E2%80%932085-243)[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHladk%C3%BDHavl%C3%AD%C4%8Dek2013-244) The concept of [homology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homology_\(biology\) "Homology (biology)") began with Aristotle;[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Panchen_1999_pp._5%E2%80%9318;_discussion_18%E2%80%9323-245) the [evolutionary developmental biologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evo-devo "Evo-devo") [Lewis I. Held](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lewis_I._Held "Lewis I. Held") commented that he would be interested in the concept of [deep homology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep_homology "Deep homology").[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Held2017-246) In [systematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Systematics "Systematics") too, recent studies suggest that Aristotle made important contributions in [taxonomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxonomy "Taxonomy") and [biological nomenclature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_nomenclature "Biological nomenclature").[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-247)[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Ganias_et_al._2017-248)[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-249)

## Surviving works

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=46 "Edit section: Surviving works")]

### Corpus Aristotelicum

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=47 "Edit section: Corpus Aristotelicum")]

Main article: [Works of Aristotle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Works_of_Aristotle "Works of Aristotle")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/82/Aristotelis_De_Moribus_ad_Nicomachum.jpg/170px-Aristotelis_De_Moribus_ad_Nicomachum.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aristotelis_De_Moribus_ad_Nicomachum.jpg)

First page of a 1566 edition of the *[Nicomachean Ethics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicomachean_Ethics "Nicomachean Ethics")* in Greek and Latin.

The works of Aristotle that have survived from antiquity through medieval manuscript transmission are collected in the Corpus Aristotelicum. These texts, as opposed to Aristotle's lost works, are technical philosophical treatises from within Aristotle's school.[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes19959-250) Reference to them is made according to the organization of [Immanuel Bekker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immanuel_Bekker "Immanuel Bekker")'s Royal Prussian Academy edition (*Aristotelis Opera edidit Academia Regia Borussica*, Berlin, 1831–1870), which in turn is based on ancient classifications of these works.[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotelis_Opera-251)

### Loss and preservation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=48 "Edit section: Loss and preservation")]

Further information: [Transmission of the Greek Classics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmission_of_the_Greek_Classics "Transmission of the Greek Classics")

Aristotle wrote his works on papyrus scrolls, the common writing medium of that era.[\[Q\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-253) His writings are divisible into two groups: the "[exoteric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exoteric "Exoteric")", intended for the public, and the "[esoteric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esoteric "Esoteric")", for use within the [Lyceum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyceum_\(classical\) "Lyceum (classical)") school.[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes199512-254)[\[R\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-255)[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHouse195635-256) Aristotle's "lost" works stray considerably in characterization from the surviving Aristotelian corpus. Whereas the lost works appear to have been originally written with a view to subsequent publication, the surviving works mostly resemble lecture notes not intended for publication.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEIrwinFine1996xi%E2%80%93xii-257)[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes199512-254) [Cicero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cicero "Cicero")'s description of Aristotle's literary style as "a river of gold" must have applied to the published works, not the surviving notes.[\[S\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-259) A major question in the history of Aristotle's works is how the exoteric writings were all lost, and how the ones now possessed came to be found.[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnesGriffin19991%E2%80%9369-260) The consensus is that Andronicus of Rhodes collected the esoteric works of Aristotle's school which existed in the form of smaller, separate works, distinguished them from those of Theophrastus and other Peripatetics, edited them, and finally compiled them into the more cohesive, larger works as they are known today.[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAnagnostopoulos201316-261)[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBarnes199510%E2%80%9315-262)

According to [Strabo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strabo "Strabo") and [Plutarch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plutarch "Plutarch"), after Aristotle's death, his library and writings went to [Theophrastus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theophrastus "Theophrastus") (Aristotle's successor as head of the Lycaeum and the [Peripatetic school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripatetic_school "Peripatetic school")).[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-263) After the death of Theophrastus, the peripatetic library went to [Neleus of Scepsis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neleus_of_Scepsis "Neleus of Scepsis").[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Grant-264): 5 

Some time later, the [Kingdom of Pergamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Pergamon "Kingdom of Pergamon") began conscripting books for a royal library, and the heirs of Neleus hid their collection in a cellar to prevent it from being seized for that purpose. The library was stored there for about a century and a half, in conditions that were not ideal for document preservation. On the death of [Attalus III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attalus_III "Attalus III"), which also ended the royal library ambitions, the existence of Aristotelian library was disclosed, and it was purchased by [Apellicon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apellicon "Apellicon") and returned to Athens in about 100 BC.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Grant-264): 5–6 

Apellicon sought to recover the texts, many of which were seriously degraded at this point due to the conditions in which they were stored. He had them copied out into new manuscripts, and used his best guesswork to fill in the gaps where the originals were unreadable.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Grant-264): 5–6 

When [Sulla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulla "Sulla") seized Athens in 86 BC, he seized the library and transferred it to Rome. There, [Andronicus of Rhodes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andronicus_of_Rhodes "Andronicus of Rhodes") organized the texts into the first complete edition of Aristotle's works (and works attributed to him).[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-265) The Aristotelian texts we have today are based on these.[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Grant-264): 6–8 

## Depictions in art

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=49 "Edit section: Depictions in art")]

### Paintings

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=50 "Edit section: Paintings")]

Aristotle has been depicted by major artists including [Lucas Cranach the Elder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucas_Cranach_the_Elder "Lucas Cranach the Elder"),[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELucas_Cranach_the_Elder-266) [Justus van Gent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justus_van_Gent "Justus van Gent"), [Raphael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raphael "Raphael"), [Paolo Veronese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paolo_Veronese "Paolo Veronese"), [Jusepe de Ribera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jusepe_de_Ribera "Jusepe de Ribera"),[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELeeRobinson2005-267) [Rembrandt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rembrandt "Rembrandt"),[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle_with_Bust2002-268) and [Francesco Hayez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francesco_Hayez "Francesco Hayez") over the centuries. Among the best-known depictions is Raphael's [fresco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fresco "Fresco") *[The School of Athens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_School_of_Athens "The School of Athens")*, in the [Vatican's Apostolic Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostolic_Palace "Apostolic Palace"), where the figures of Plato and Aristotle are central to the image, at the architectural [vanishing point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vanishing_point "Vanishing point"), reflecting their importance.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPhelan2002-269) Rembrandt's *[Aristotle with a Bust of Homer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_with_a_Bust_of_Homer "Aristotle with a Bust of Homer")*, too, is a celebrated work, showing the knowing philosopher and the blind Homer from an earlier age: as the art critic [Jonathan Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Jones_\(journalist\) "Jonathan Jones (journalist)") writes, "this painting will remain one of the greatest and most mysterious in the world, ensnaring us in its musty, glowing, pitch-black, terrible knowledge of time."[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeld1969-270)[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJones2002-271)

## Eponyms

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Aristotle\&action=edit\&section=52 "Edit section: Eponyms")]

The [Aristotle Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristotle_Mountains "Aristotle Mountains") in [Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antarctica "Antarctica") are named after Aristotle. He was the first person known to conjecture, in his book *[Meteorology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meteorology_\(Aristotle\) "Meteorology (Aristotle)")*, the existence of a landmass in the southern high-latitude region, which he called *Antarctica*.[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristotle_Mountains-273) [Aristoteles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aristoteles_\(crater\) "Aristoteles (crater)") is a crater on the [Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon "Moon") bearing the classical form of Aristotle's name.[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAristoteles-274) [(6123) Aristoteles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/6123_Aristoteles "6123 Aristoteles"), an [asteroid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid "Asteroid") in the [main asteroid belt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asteroid_belt "Asteroid belt") is also bearing the classical form of his name.[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-275)

3. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-4)** See [Shields 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#CITEREFShields2012), pp. 3–16. [Blits 1999](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#CITEREFBlits1999), p. 58 writes that most information about Aristotle's life derives from [Diogenes Laertius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diogenes_Laertius "Diogenes Laertius")' *[Lives and Opinions of Eminent Philosophers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lives_and_Opinions_of_Eminent_Philosophers "Lives and Opinions of Eminent Philosophers")*, which in turn borrows material from earlier, now mostly lost, sources. [Düring 1957](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#CITEREFD%C3%BCring1957) covers ancient biographies of Aristotle.
4. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-5)** That these dates (the first half of the Olympiad year 384/383 BC, and in 322 shortly before the death of Demosthenes) are correct was shown by [August Boeckh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/August_Boeckh "August Boeckh") (*Kleine Schriften* VI 195); for further discussion, see [Felix Jacoby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Felix_Jacoby "Felix Jacoby") on *[FGrHist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FGrHist "FGrHist")* 244 F 38. Ingemar Düring, *Aristotle in the Ancient Biographical Tradition*, Göteborg, 1957, p. 253
5. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-21)** [Nussbaum & Osborne 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#CITEREFNussbaumOsborne2014), p. 73 write that Hermias died in the year 345 BC; [Hazel 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#CITEREFHazel2013), p. 37 places Hermias' death in 342 BC, the same year as Aristotle's trip back to Macedon, while [Nawotka 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#CITEREFNawotka2009), p. 40 mentions that Hermias got arrested in 341 BC.
6. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-59)** This [type of syllogism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syllogism#Types "Syllogism"), with all three terms in 'a', is known by the traditional (medieval) [mnemonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mnemonic "Mnemonic") [Barbara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baralipton "Baralipton").[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELagerlund2016-58)
7. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-60)** M is the Middle (here, Men), S is the Subject (Greeks), P is the Predicate (mortal).[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTELagerlund2016-58)
8. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-62)** The first equation can be read as 'It is not true that there exists an x such that x is a man and that x is not mortal.'[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPredicate_Logic-61)
9. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-72)** Rhett Allain notes that [Newton's First Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newton%27s_First_Law "Newton's First Law") is "essentially a direct reply to Aristotle, that the natural state is *not to change* motion.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAllain2016-71)
10. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-75)** Leonard Susskind comments that Aristotle had clearly never gone [ice skating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_skating "Ice skating") or he would have seen that it takes force to stop an object.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTESusskind2011-74)
11. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-77)** For heavenly bodies like the Sun, Moon, and stars, the observed motions are "to a very good approximation" circular around the Earth's centre, (for example, the apparent rotation of the sky because of the rotation of the Earth, and the rotation of the moon around the Earth) as Aristotle stated.[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)
12. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-78)** Drabkin quotes numerous passages from *Physics* and *On the Heavens* (*De Caelo*) which state Aristotle's laws of motion.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73)
13. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-79)** Drabkin agrees that density is treated quantitatively in this passage, but without a sharp definition of density as weight per unit volume.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDrabkin193860%E2%80%9384-73)
14. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-81)** Philoponus and Galileo correctly objected that for the transient phase (still increasing in speed) with heavy objects falling a short distance, the law does not apply: Galileo used balls on a short incline to show this. Rovelli notes that "Two heavy balls with the same shape and different weight do fall at different speeds from an aeroplane, confirming Aristotle's theory, not Galileo's."[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERovelli201523%E2%80%9340-76)
15. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-159)** For a different reading of social and economic processes in the *Nicomachean Ethics* and *Politics* see Polanyi, Karl (1957) "Aristotle Discovers the Economy" in *Primitive, Archaic and Modern Economies: Essays of Karl Polanyi* ed. G. Dalton, Boston 1971, 78–115.
16. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-179)** "Where, as among the Lacedaemonians, the state of women is bad, almost half of human life is spoilt."[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTERhetoricBook_I,_Chapter_5-178)
17. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-253)** "When the Roman dictator Sulla invaded Athens in 86 BC, he brought back to Rome a fantastic prize – Aristotle's library. Books then were papyrus rolls, from 10 to 20 feet long, and since Aristotle's death in 322 BC, worms and damp had done their worst. The rolls needed repairing, and the texts clarifying and copying on to new papyrus (imported from Egypt – Moses' bulrushes). The man in Rome who put Aristotle's library in order was a Greek scholar, Tyrannio."[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWhen_libraries_were2001-252)
18. **[^](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_ref-255)** Aristotle: *Nicomachean Ethics* 1102a26–27. Aristotle himself never uses the term "esoteric" or "acroamatic". For other passages where Aristotle speaks of *exōterikoi logoi*, see [W.D. Ross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/W.D._Ross "W.D. Ross"), *Aristotle's Metaphysics* (1953), vol. 2 pp= 408–410. Ross defends an interpretation according to which the phrase, at least in Aristotle's own works, usually refers generally to "discussions not peculiar to the [Peripatetic school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peripatetic_school "Peripatetic school")", rather than to specific works of Aristotle's own.
